I don’t know if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you have control over what you're eating at night when this happens?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean… I’d like to say I’m choosing it, but once I start at night it feels like I’m watching myself do it. I’ll be thinking, “Okay, stop now,” but then I’m still reaching for more anyway. It’s embarrassing, but that’s how it feels.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty after these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I… I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly. Maybe a little embarrassed, I guess. I mostly try not to think too much about it afterward, if that makes sense. I know that probably sounds a bit evasive, but… I’m not really sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating without control happen only at night or do they also happen during the day?\n\nPatient: Mostly at night, I think. I mean, that’s the time I really notice it—that feeling where once I start eating, I’m sort of watching myself and telling myself to stop, but I keep reaching for more anyway. During the day… I don’t think it happens like that, not in the same way. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you remember everything that happens during these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think I do, mostly. I’m not blacking out or anything like that. It’s more that afterward I don’t really like replaying it in my head, so some of it gets kind of fuzzy because I’m avoiding thinking about it. But if I stop and try, I can usually remember what happened. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat what most people would consider a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, probably. I don’t like saying it that way, but some evenings I’ll start with just leftovers, and then it turns into a couple sandwiches, cereal, maybe cookies, and whatever snack things are around in the pantry. It’s definitely more than I’d ever put on a plate and call a normal supper. Is that the kind of amount you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control and eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a similar time frame?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. It’s not just that I eat more than I should. Once I start at night, it feels like I’m watching myself do it and I’m telling myself, “Okay, stop now,” but then my hands just keep going back for more.\n\nI know that sounds bad, but that’s what brings me in, I guess. It feels bigger than what most people would eat in that amount of time, and I don’t feel very in control of it when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or disturbed by these episodes of eating large amounts of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I don’t feel good about it afterward. I try not to make too much of it, because I know people overeat sometimes, but this feels different. I get pretty ashamed and disappointed in myself, and I usually wish I could just undo it.\n\nI don’t know if “disturbed” is the right word, but it definitely bothers me. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find evidence of eating or preparing food that you don't remember doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like finding a mess and having no memory of making it. I mean, sometimes the kitchen looks worse than I’d like to admit afterward, but I do remember being there and eating.\n\nUnless you mean something different by “evidence”? I haven’t noticed anything that felt like I blacked out or didn’t know I’d done it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Maybe a little embarrassed, I guess. I mostly try not to think too much about it afterward, if that makes sense. I know that probably sounds a bit evasive, but… I’m not really sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating without control happen only at night or do they also happen during the day?\n\nPatient: Mostly at night, I think. I mean, that’s the time I really notice it—that feeling where once I start eating, I’m sort of watching myself and telling myself to stop, but I keep reaching for more anyway. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you remember everything that happens during these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think I do, mostly. I’m not blacking out or anything like that. It’s more that afterward I don’t really like replaying it in my head, so some of it gets kind of fuzzy because I’m avoiding thinking about it. But if I stop and try, I can usually remember what happened. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat what most people would consider a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, probably. I don’t like saying it that way, but some evenings I’ll start with just leftovers, and then it turns into a couple sandwiches, cereal, maybe cookies, and whatever snack things are around in the pantry. Is that the kind of amount you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control and eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a similar time frame?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. Once I start at night, it feels like I’m watching myself do it and I’m telling myself, “Okay, stop now,” but then my hands just keep going back for more.\n\nI know that sounds bad, but that’s what brings me in, I guess. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find evidence of eating or preparing food that you don't remember doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like finding a mess and having no memory of making it. I know that probably sounds a bit evasive, but… I’m not really sure how to answer that.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating without control happen only at night or do they also happen during the day?\n\nPatient: Mostly at night, I think. I mean, that’s the time I really notice it—that feeling where once I start eating, I’m sort of watching myself and telling myself to stop, but I keep reaching for more anyway. During the day… I don’t think it happens like that, not in the same way. Does that answer what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you remember everything that happens during these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think I do, mostly. I’m not blacking out or anything like that. It’s more that afterward I don’t really like replaying it in my head, so some of it gets kind of fuzzy because I’m avoiding thinking about it. But if I stop and try, I can usually remember what happened. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat what most people would consider a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, probably. I don’t like saying it that way, but some evenings I’ll start with just leftovers, and then it turns into a couple sandwiches, cereal, maybe cookies, and whatever snack things are around in the pantry. It’s definitely more than I’d ever put on a plate and call a normal supper. Is that the kind of amount you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control and eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a similar time frame?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. It’s not just that I eat more than I should. Once I start at night, it feels like I’m watching myself do it and I’m telling myself, “Okay, stop now,” but then my hands just keep going back for more.\n\nI know that sounds bad, but that’s what brings me in, I guess. It feels bigger than what most people would eat in that amount of time, and I don’t feel very in control of it when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or disturbed by these episodes of eating large amounts of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I don’t feel good about it afterward. I try not to make too much of it, because I know people overeat sometimes, but this feels different. I get pretty ashamed and disappointed in myself, and I usually wish I could just undo it.\n\nI don’t know if “disturbed” is the right word, but it definitely bothers me. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find evidence of eating or preparing food that you don't remember doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like finding a mess and having no memory of making it. I mean, sometimes the kitchen looks worse than I’d like to admit afterward, but I do remember being there and eating.\n\nUnless you mean something different by “evidence”? I haven’t noticed anything that felt like I blacked out or didn’t know I’d done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time, and during those episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating even though you might want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much it. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but what brings me in is that once I start eating at night, it’s like I can see myself doing it and I’m telling myself, “Stop, that’s enough,” and then my hands just keep going back for more.\n\nSo yes, I do want to stop in the moment, but I don’t seem to be able to, at least not right away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel guilty or remorseful immediately after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t feel proud of it, definitely. There’s usually this “why did I do that?” feeling right after, and I kind of want to move past it quickly rather than sit with it.\n\nSo, yes, I guess guilt is there, though I try not to make a big scene of it in my head. Remorseful might be a strong word, but I do feel bad afterward. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are fully awake and aware of your surroundings during these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think I am, yes. I mean, I’m not wandering around half-asleep or anything like that. I know I’m in the kitchen, I know what I’m eating, I know what time it is… more or less.\n\nIt’s more that I feel kind of caught up in it, if that makes sense, not unaware. I’m conscious of what’s happening, even if I’m not making the best choices in the moment. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember some parts of what happened during these episodes of eating at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of blacking out or having gaps. I usually remember going to the kitchen and what I was doing.\n\nMaybe the details get a little fuzzy, like exactly how much or the order of things, but I wouldn’t say I don’t remember parts of it. I’m aware enough that afterward I know what happened. Does that answer it?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, that’s the time I really notice it—that feeling where once I start eating, I’m sort of watching myself and telling myself to stop, but I keep reaching for more anyway. I try not to make too much of it, because I know people overeat sometimes, but this feels different. I’m conscious of what’s happening, even if I’m not making the best choices in the moment. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember some parts of what happened during these episodes of eating at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of blacking out or having gaps. I usually remember going to the kitchen and what I was doing.\n\nMaybe the details get a little fuzzy, like exactly how much or the order of things, but I wouldn’t say I don’t remember parts of it. I’m aware enough that afterward I know what happened. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat what most people would consider a large amount of food in a short period of time during these episodes?\n\nPatient: I guess… yes, probably. I don’t like saying it that way, but some evenings I’ll start with just leftovers, and then it turns into a couple sandwiches, cereal, maybe cookies, and whatever snack things are around in the pantry. It’s definitely more than I’d ever put on a plate and call a normal supper. Is that the kind of amount you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control and eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a similar time frame?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. It’s not just that I eat more than I should. Once I start at night, it feels like I’m watching myself do it and I’m telling myself, “Okay, stop now,” but then my hands just keep going back for more.\n\nI know that sounds bad, but that’s what brings me in, I guess. It feels bigger than what most people would eat in that amount of time, and I don’t feel very in control of it when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or disturbed by these episodes of eating large amounts of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I don’t feel good about it afterward. I try not to make too much of it, because I know people overeat sometimes, but this feels different. I get pretty ashamed and disappointed in myself, and I usually wish I could just undo it.\n\nI don’t know if “disturbed” is the right word, but it definitely bothers me. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find evidence of eating or preparing food that you don't remember doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like finding a mess and having no memory of making it. I mean, sometimes the kitchen looks worse than I’d like to admit afterward, but I do remember being there and eating.\n\nUnless you mean something different by “evidence”? I haven’t noticed anything that felt like I blacked out or didn’t know I’d done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time, and during those episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating even though you might want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much it. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but what brings me in is that once I start eating at night, it’s like I can see myself doing it and I’m telling myself, “Stop, that’s enough,” and then my hands just keep going back for more.\n\nSo yes, I do want to stop in the moment, but I don’t seem to be able to, at least not right away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel guilty or remorseful immediately after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t feel proud of it, definitely. There’s usually this “why did I do that?” feeling right after, and I kind of want to move past it quickly rather than sit with it.\n\nSo, yes, I guess guilt is there, though I try not to make a big scene of it in my head. Remorseful might be a strong word, but I do feel bad afterward. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are fully awake and aware of your surroundings during these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think I am, yes. I mean, I’m not wandering around half-asleep or anything like that. I know I’m in the kitchen, I know what I’m eating, I know what time it is… more or less.\n\nIt’s more that I feel kind of caught up in it, if that makes sense, not unaware. I’m conscious of what’s happening, even if I’m not making the best choices in the moment. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember some parts of what happened during these episodes of eating at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of blacking out or having gaps. I usually remember going to the kitchen and what I was doing.\n\nMaybe the details get a little fuzzy, like exactly how much or the order of things, but I wouldn’t say I don’t remember parts of it. I’m aware enough that afterward I know what happened. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten things that aren't food during these episodes, like dirt, paper, or other non-food items?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, nothing like dirt or paper or anything that isn’t actually food.\n\nIt’s more just… regular food, even if I’m not exactly proud of the amount or the timing. But no, I haven’t noticed myself eating non-food things. Is that what you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever wake up in the morning and find evidence that you ate the night before, but have no memory of eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think that’s happened. I haven’t woken up and found wrappers or dishes or something and had no idea how they got there.\n\nIf I ate during the night, I pretty much remember it, even if I’d rather not think about it too much in the morning. But not a total blank, no. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember eating at night until you see evidence of it the next morning?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, if I ate at night, I’m usually aware of it at the time, even if I don’t feel great about it later.\n\nI haven’t had that thing where I only realize it because I see crumbs or wrappers in the morning and then it comes back to me. At least not that I’ve noticed. Is that okay—does that answer what you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

{"intent": "determine if the patient experiences a loss of recall for the eating episodes", "question": "Do you ever find that you don't remember some parts of what happened during these episodes of eating at night?"}

It feels bigger than what most people would eat in that amount of time, and I don’t feel very in control of it when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or disturbed by these episodes of eating large amounts of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I don’t feel good about it afterward. I try not to make too much of it, because I know people overeat sometimes, but this feels different. I get pretty ashamed and disappointed in myself, and I usually wish I could just undo it.\n\nI don’t know if “disturbed” is the right word, but it definitely bothers me. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find evidence of eating or preparing food that you don't remember doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like finding a mess and having no memory of making it. I mean, sometimes the kitchen looks worse than I’d like to admit afterward, but I do remember being there and eating.\n\nUnless you mean something different by “evidence”? I haven’t noticed anything that felt like I blacked out or didn’t know I’d done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time, and during those episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating even though you might want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much it. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but what brings me in is that once I start eating at night, it’s like I can see myself doing it and I’m telling myself, “Stop, that’s enough,” and then my hands just keep going back for more.\n\nSo yes, I do want to stop in the moment, but I don’t seem to be able to, at least not right away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel guilty or remorseful immediately after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t feel proud of it, definitely. There’s usually this “why did I do that?” feeling right after, and I kind of want to move past it quickly rather than sit with it.\n\nSo, yes, I guess guilt is there, though I try not to make a big scene of it in my head. Remorseful might be a strong word, but I do feel bad afterward. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are fully awake and aware of your surroundings during these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think I am, yes. I mean, I’m not wandering around half-asleep or anything like that. I know I’m in the kitchen, I know what I’m eating, I know what time it is… more or less.\n\nIt’s more that I feel kind of caught up in it, if that makes sense, not unaware. I’m conscious of what’s happening, even if I’m not making the best choices in the moment. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember some parts of what happened during these episodes of eating at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of blacking out or having gaps. I usually remember going to the kitchen and what I was doing.\n\nMaybe the details get a little fuzzy, like exactly how much or the order of things, but I wouldn’t say I don’t remember parts of it. I’m aware enough that afterward I know what happened. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten things that aren't food during these episodes, like dirt, paper, or other non-food items?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, nothing like dirt or paper or anything that isn’t actually food.\n\nIt’s more just… regular food, even if I’m not exactly proud of the amount or the timing. But no, I haven’t noticed myself eating non-food things. Is that what you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever wake up in the morning and find evidence that you ate the night before, but have no memory of eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think that’s happened. I haven’t woken up and found wrappers or dishes or something and had no idea how they got there.\n\nIf I ate during the night, I pretty much remember it, even if I’d rather not think about it too much in the morning. But not a total blank, no. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember eating at night until you see evidence of it the next morning?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, if I ate at night, I’m usually aware of it at the time, even if I don’t feel great about it later.\n\nI haven’t had that thing where I only realize it because I see crumbs or wrappers in the morning and then it comes back to me. At least not that I’ve noticed. Is that okay—does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember some parts of what happened during these episodes of eating at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of missing chunks of what happened. I may be a little hazy on exact details, like how much time passed or every single thing I ate, but I’m generally aware of what I’m doing at the time.\n\nI haven’t noticed blackouts or anything like that. I hope that’s answering it the way you mean.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

It feels bigger than what most people would eat in that amount of time, and I don’t feel very in control of it when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or disturbed by these episodes of eating large amounts of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I don’t feel good about it afterward. I try not to make too much of it, because I know people overeat sometimes, but this feels different. I get pretty ashamed and disappointed in myself, and I usually wish I could just undo it.\n\nI don’t know if “disturbed” is the right word, but it definitely bothers me. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find evidence of eating or preparing food that you don't remember doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like finding a mess and having no memory of making it. I mean, sometimes the kitchen looks worse than I’d like to admit afterward, but I do remember being there and eating.\n\nUnless you mean something different by “evidence”? I haven’t noticed anything that felt like I blacked out or didn’t know I’d done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time, and during those episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating even though you might want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much it. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but what brings me in is that once I start eating at night, it’s like I can see myself doing it and I’m telling myself, “Stop, that’s enough,” and then my hands just keep going back for more.\n\nSo yes, I do want to stop in the moment, but I don’t seem to be able to, at least not right away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel guilty or remorseful immediately after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t feel proud of it, definitely. There’s usually this “why did I do that?” feeling right after, and I kind of want to move past it quickly rather than sit with it.\n\nSo, yes, I guess guilt is there, though I try not to make a big scene of it in my head. Remorseful might be a strong word, but I do feel bad afterward. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are fully awake and aware of your surroundings during these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think I am, yes. I mean, I’m not wandering around half-asleep or anything like that. I know I’m in the kitchen, I know what I’m eating, I know what time it is… more or less.\n\nIt’s more that I feel kind of caught up in it, if that makes sense, not unaware. I’m conscious of what’s happening, even if I’m not making the best choices in the moment. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember some parts of what happened during these episodes of eating at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of blacking out or having gaps. I usually remember going to the kitchen and what I was doing.\n\nMaybe the details get a little fuzzy, like exactly how much or the order of things, but I wouldn’t say I don’t remember parts of it. I’m aware enough that afterward I know what happened. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten things that aren't food during these episodes, like dirt, paper, or other non-food items?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, nothing like dirt or paper or anything that isn’t actually food.\n\nIt’s more just… regular food, even if I’m not exactly proud of the amount or the timing. But no, I haven’t noticed myself eating non-food things. Is that what you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever wake up in the morning and find evidence that you ate the night before, but have no memory of eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think that’s happened. I haven’t woken up and found wrappers or dishes or something and had no idea how they got there.\n\nIf I ate during the night, I pretty much remember it, even if I’d rather not think about it too much in the morning. But not a total blank, no. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember eating at night until you see evidence of it the next morning?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, if I ate at night, I’m usually aware of it at the time, even if I don’t feel great about it later.\n\nI haven’t had that thing where I only realize it because I see crumbs or wrappers in the morning and then it comes back to me. At least not that I’ve noticed. Is that okay—does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember some parts of what happened during these episodes of eating at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of missing chunks of what happened. I may be a little hazy on exact details, like how much time passed or every single thing I ate, but I’m generally aware of what I’m doing at the time.\n\nI haven’t noticed blackouts or anything like that. I hope that’s answering it the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can't remember what you ate during these nighttime episodes after they're over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I might not remember every little thing exactly, like whether it was two of something or three, but I usually have a general sense of what I ate afterward.\n\nIt’s not like it’s a blank or I have to piece it together later. At least, I haven’t noticed that happening. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t like saying it that way, but some evenings I’ll start with just leftovers, and then it turns into a couple sandwiches, cereal, maybe cookies, and whatever snack things are around in the pantry. It’s definitely more than I’d ever put on a plate and call a normal supper. Is that the kind of amount you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control and eaten an amount of food that's much larger than what most people would eat in a similar time frame?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. It’s not just that I eat more than I should. Once I start at night, it feels like I’m watching myself do it and I’m telling myself, “Okay, stop now,” but then my hands just keep going back for more.\n\nI know that sounds bad, but that’s what brings me in, I guess. It feels bigger than what most people would eat in that amount of time, and I don’t feel very in control of it when it’s happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel upset or disturbed by these episodes of eating large amounts of food in a short time?\n\nPatient: I mean… yes, I don’t feel good about it afterward. I try not to make too much of it, because I know people overeat sometimes, but this feels different. I get pretty ashamed and disappointed in myself, and I usually wish I could just undo it.\n\nI don’t know if “disturbed” is the right word, but it definitely bothers me. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find evidence of eating or preparing food that you don't remember doing?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not like finding a mess and having no memory of making it. I mean, sometimes the kitchen looks worse than I’d like to admit afterward, but I do remember being there and eating.\n\nUnless you mean something different by “evidence”? I haven’t noticed anything that felt like I blacked out or didn’t know I’d done it.\n\nDoctor: Do you have episodes where you eat a large amount of food in a short time, and during those episodes, do you feel like you're unable to stop eating even though you might want to?\n\nPatient: Yes… that’s pretty much it. I don’t want to make it sound more dramatic than it is, but what brings me in is that once I start eating at night, it’s like I can see myself doing it and I’m telling myself, “Stop, that’s enough,” and then my hands just keep going back for more.\n\nSo yes, I do want to stop in the moment, but I don’t seem to be able to, at least not right away. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you typically feel guilty or remorseful immediately after these episodes of eating large amounts of food?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I mean… I don’t feel proud of it, definitely. There’s usually this “why did I do that?” feeling right after, and I kind of want to move past it quickly rather than sit with it.\n\nSo, yes, I guess guilt is there, though I try not to make a big scene of it in my head. Remorseful might be a strong word, but I do feel bad afterward. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you are fully awake and aware of your surroundings during these nighttime eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I think I am, yes. I mean, I’m not wandering around half-asleep or anything like that. I know I’m in the kitchen, I know what I’m eating, I know what time it is… more or less.\n\nIt’s more that I feel kind of caught up in it, if that makes sense, not unaware. I’m conscious of what’s happening, even if I’m not making the best choices in the moment. Is that what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember some parts of what happened during these episodes of eating at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of blacking out or having gaps. I usually remember going to the kitchen and what I was doing.\n\nMaybe the details get a little fuzzy, like exactly how much or the order of things, but I wouldn’t say I don’t remember parts of it. I’m aware enough that afterward I know what happened. Does that answer it?\n\nDoctor: Have you ever eaten things that aren't food during these episodes, like dirt, paper, or other non-food items?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, nothing like dirt or paper or anything that isn’t actually food.\n\nIt’s more just… regular food, even if I’m not exactly proud of the amount or the timing. But no, I haven’t noticed myself eating non-food things. Is that what you meant?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever wake up in the morning and find evidence that you ate the night before, but have no memory of eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think that’s happened. I haven’t woken up and found wrappers or dishes or something and had no idea how they got there.\n\nIf I ate during the night, I pretty much remember it, even if I’d rather not think about it too much in the morning. But not a total blank, no. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember eating at night until you see evidence of it the next morning?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. I mean, if I ate at night, I’m usually aware of it at the time, even if I don’t feel great about it later.\n\nI haven’t had that thing where I only realize it because I see crumbs or wrappers in the morning and then it comes back to me. At least not that I’ve noticed. Is that okay—does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find that you don't remember some parts of what happened during these episodes of eating at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of missing chunks of what happened. I may be a little hazy on exact details, like how much time passed or every single thing I ate, but I’m generally aware of what I’m doing at the time.\n\nI haven’t noticed blackouts or anything like that. I hope that’s answering it the way you mean.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you can't remember what you ate during these nighttime episodes after they're over?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not really. I mean, I might not remember every little thing exactly, like whether it was two of something or three, but I usually have a general sense of what I ate afterward.\n\nIt’s not like it’s a blank or I have to piece it together later. At least, I haven’t noticed that happening. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat at night and then later realize you did it, but have no memory of actually eating?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think that’s happened. If I eat at night, I’m aware of doing it at the time… even if afterward I’d rather not think about it too much.\n\nBut I don’t have that experience of realizing later that I ate and having no memory of it. At least not that I’ve noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.
